B206: Foreword
B209: This Technical Report has been produced by the 3rd Generation Partnership Project (3GPP).
B210: The contents of the present document are subject to continuing work within the TSG and may change following formal TSG approval. Should the TSG modify the contents of the present document, it will be re-released by the TSG with an identifying change of release date and an increase in version number as follows:
B211: Version x.y.z
B212: where:
B213: xthe first digit:
B214: 1presented to TSG for information;
B215: 2presented to TSG for approval;
B216: 3or greater indicates TSG approved document under change control.
B217: ythe second digit is incremented for all changes of substance, i.e. technical enhancements, corrections, updates, etc.
B218: zthe third digit is incremented when editorial only changes have been incorporated in the document.
B219: In the present document, modal verbs have the following meanings:
B220: shallindicates a mandatory requirement to do something
B221: shall notindicates an interdiction (prohibition) to do something
B222: The constructions "shall" and "shall not" are confined to the context of normative provisions, and do not appear in Technical Reports.
B223: The constructions "must" and "must not" are not used as substitutes for "shall" and "shall not". Their use is avoided insofar as possible, and they are not used in a normative context except in a direct citation from an external, referenced, non-3GPP document, or so as to maintain continuity of style when extending or modifying the provisions of such a referenced document.
B224: shouldindicates a recommendation to do something
B225: should notindicates a recommendation not to do something
B226: mayindicates permission to do something
B227: need notindicates permission not to do something
B228: The construction "may not" is ambiguous and is not used in normative elements. The unambiguous constructions "might not" or "shall not" are used instead, depending upon the meaning intended.
B229: canindicates that something is possible
B230: cannotindicates that something is impossible
B231: The constructions "can" and "cannot" are not substitutes for "may" and "need not".
B232: willindicates that something is certain or expected to happen as a result of action taken by an agency the behaviour of which is outside the scope of the present document
B233: will notindicates that something is certain or expected not to happen as a result of action taken by an agency the behaviour of which is outside the scope of the present document
B234: mightindicates a likelihood that something will happen as a result of action taken by some agency the behaviour of which is outside the scope of the present document
B235: might notindicates a likelihood that something will not happen as a result of action taken by some agency the behaviour of which is outside the scope of the present document
B236: In addition:
B237: is(or any other verb in the indicative mood) indicates a statement of fact
B238: is not(or any other negative verb in the indicative mood) indicates a statement of fact
B239: The constructions "is" and "is not" do not indicate requirements.
B240: 1Scope
B241: The present document is intended to capture the output of study item for " Study on low-power wake up signal and receiver for NR" [2]. 
B242: The study includes investigations to 
B243: -the impact of low-power wake-up signal and receiver , including power saving benefit, coverage, system overhead impact, network energy impact and other related aspects.
B244: -the receiver architecture for low-power wake-up receiver and provide analysis for power consumption, noise figure and etc.
B245: -L1 designs and procedures changes needed to support the wake-up signal and evaluations for the link performances.
B246: -higher layer protocol changes needed to support the wake-up signals
B247: -related RAN4 impacts
B248: 2References
B249: The following documents contain provisions which, through reference in this text, constitute provisions of the present document.
B250: -References are either specific (identified by date of publication, edition number, version number, etc.) or nonspecific.
B251: -For a specific reference, subsequent revisions do not apply.
B252: -For a non-specific reference, the latest version applies. In the case of a reference to a 3GPP document (including a GSM document), a non-specific reference implicitly refers to the latest version of that document in the same Release as the present document.

B403: 4Introduction
B404: 5G systems are designed and developed targeting for both mobile telephony and vertical use cases. Besides latency, reliability, and availability, UE energy efficiency is also critical to 5G. Currently, 5G devices may have to be recharged per week or day, depending on individual's usage time. In general, 5G devices consume tens of milliwatts in RRC idle/inactive state and hundreds of milliwatts in RRC connected state. Designs to prolong battery life is a necessity for improving energy efficiency as well as for better user experience. 
B405: Energy efficiency is even more critical for UEs without a continuous energy source, e.g., UEs using small rechargeable and single coin cell batteries. Among vertical use cases, sensors and actuators are deployed extensively for monitoring, measuring, charging, etc. Generally, their batteries are not rechargeable and expected to last at least few years as described in TR 38.875. Wearables include smart watches, rings, eHealth related devices, and medical monitoring devices. With typical battery capacity, it is challenging to sustain up to 1-2 weeks as required. 
B406: The power consumption depends on the configured length of wake-up periods, e.g., paging cycle. To meet the battery life requirements above, eDRX cycle with large value is expected to be used, resulting in high latency, which is not suitable for such services with requirements of both long battery life and low latency. For example, in fire detection and extinguishment use case, fire shutters shall be closed and fire sprinklers shall be turned on by the actuators within 1 to 2 seconds from the time the fire is detected by sensors, long eDRX cycle cannot meet the delay requirements. eDRX is apparently not suitable for latency-critical use cases. Thus, the intention is to study ultra-low power mechanism that can support low latency in Rel-18, e.g. lower than eDRX latency.
B407: Currently, UEs need to periodically wake up once per DRX cycle, which dominates the power consumption in periods with no signalling or data traffic. If UEs are able to wake up only when they are triggered, e.g., paging, power consumption could be dramatically reduced. This can be achieved by using a wake-up signal to trigger the main radio and a separate receiver which has the ability to monitor wake-up signal with ultra-low power consumption. Main radio works for data transmission and reception, which can be turned off or set to deep sleep unless it is turned on.
B408: The power consumption for monitoring wake-up signal depends on the wake-up signal design and the hardware module of the wake-up receiver used for signal detecting and processing. 
B409: The study should primarily target low-power WUS/WUR for power-sensitive, small form-factor devices including IoT use cases (such as industrial sensors, controllers) and wearables. Other use cases are not precluded, e.g.XR/smart glasses, smart phones. 
B410: As opposed to the work on UE power savings in previous releases, this study will not require existing signals to be used as WUS. All WUS solutions identified shall be able to operate in a cell supporting legacy UEs. Solutions should target substantial gains compared to the existing Rel-15/16/17 UE power saving mechanisms. Other aspects such as detection performance, coverage, UE complexity, should be covered by the evaluation.
B411: The study item includes the following objectives:
B412: -Identify evaluation methodology (including the use cases) & KPIs [RAN1]
B413: -Primarily target low-power WUS/WUR for power-sensitive, small form-factor devices including IoT use cases (such as industrial sensors, controllers) and wearables
B414: -Other use cases are not precluded
B415: -Study and evaluate low-power wake-up receiver architectures [RAN1, RAN4] 
B416: -Study and evaluate wake-up signal designs to support wake-up receivers [RAN1, RAN4] 
B417: -Study and evaluate L1 procedures and higher layer protocol changes needed to support the wake-up signals [RAN2, RAN1] 
B418: -Study potential UE power saving gains compared to the existing Rel-15/16/17 UE power saving mechanisms, the coverage availability, as well as latency impact of low-power WUR/WUS. System impact, such as network power consumption, coexistence with non-low-power-WUR UEs, network coverage/capacity/resource overhead should be included in the study [RAN1]
B419: -Note: The need for RAN2 evaluation will be triggered by RAN1 when necessary. 
B420: Use the following terminology for future discussion,
B421: -Main radio (MR): the Tx/Rx module operating for NR signals/channels apart from signals/channel related to low-power wake-up
B422: -LP-WUR (LR): The Rx module operating for receiving/processing signals/channel related to low-power wake-up.

B443: The latency for LP-WUS/WUR are considered as follows
B444: -Latency for RRC CONNECTED mode can be up to tens of milliseconds. 
B445: -Latency for RRC IDLE/INACTIVE mode can be in the order of seconds, for I-DRX use cases, and the latency can be larger for eDRX use cases.
B446: Both RRC IDLE/INACTIVE and CONNECTED modes were studied as part of the LP-WUS/WUR SI.

FFS FAR requirement based on the study outcome of the impact of FAR on power consumption / power saving gain / system overhead . The FAR definition does NOT include the impact of the falsely alarmed for wake-up due to the detection of a LP-WUS which is intended to wake-up/alarm the LP-WUR of another UE within the same UE group . For evaluation purpose, FAR target is determined across a reference time duration T of one or multiple LP-WUS attempts/trials, - UE  has  N attempts within T,  - Company to report (FAR target, T, N) - For example,  - if UE makes a single decision based on multiple correlations for a sequence in the monitor occasion, these correlations are considered as UE implementation in ONE trial/attempt. - if UE performs decoding in a monitor occasion, a single decoding is considered as ONE trial/attempt. - If UE performs N non-overlap attempts within the reference time duration, the false alarm event for the attempts are assumed as independent. Companies   to   provide the assumed side conditions to attain the used FAR over T or per one attempt e.g. CRC/sequence length in LP-WUS design.

B537: -The following are examples for consideration, other approaches are not precluded,
B538: -Model 1:
B539: -The relationship between a drifted frequency error(ΔF), frequency drift ( F') over a time (T1) is ΔF = ±F' * T1
B540: -When frequency displacement [Fd] reaches max frequency error, it is assumed to be equaled to max frequency error
B541: -T1 is the time from the previous frequency synchronization. T1 may take different values depending on the chosen frequency synchronization approach.
B542: -For Model 1 of frequency error, Frequency displacement (Fd), defined as the difference between ideal frequency and frequency due to 1) clock drifting (ΔF); and 2) residual frequency error from previous synchronization/calibration (Fr), is given as Fd (ppm)=ΔF (ppm) +Fr(ppm), 
B543: -Companies to report Fr and important assumptions for achieving Fr, e.g., if MR can assist to calibrate LP-WUR to correct the frequency error or if LP-WUR can only correct the frequency error based on LP-WUS synchronization signal
B544: -Company to report the timing drifting error assumption for the detection of LP-WUS/synchronization signal,
B545: -The following are examples for consideration, other approaches are not precluded,
B546: -Model 1 [R1-2301438] [R1-2301558][R1-1714993]:
B547: -The relationship between the maximum frequency error(Fe) and corresponding timing drift( ΔT) over a time(T) is ΔT = ±Fe * T (linear region)
B548: -The relationship between a frequency drift( F'), and corresponding timing drift(ΔT) over a time(T) is ΔT = Fr*T ±0.5 * F' *T2 (transient region)
B549: -The transition between transient and linear region (from synchronization or calibration point/time) occurs at time [Ts= (Fe-Fr)/( F')]
B550: -
B551: -T is the time from the previous time synchronization. T may take different values depending on the chosen synchronization approach
B552: -FFS: Time error (Te) before detection of a current sync signal is defined as the difference between ideal time of the current sync signal and the time error due to 1) clock time drift (ΔT); and 2) residual time error from previous synchronization/calibration (Tr); Te= ΔT+ Tr
B554: The following for usage of the clock is assumed for LP-WUR OFF/ON.
B555: Table 6.2-5: Assumptions for the usage of the LP-WUR clock
B556 R0: Assumption on LP-WUR OFF power | Assumptions on the clock usage
B556 R1: 0.001 | When LP-WUR is OFF - Time offset cumulated in the off period cannot be calculated based on the parameters of the oscillator option 1/2/3/4. RTC should be used(Only RTC is running during sleep.) When LP-WUR is ON, frequency offset and time offset calculation can follow the parameters of the oscillator option 1/2/3/4  (cumulating based on the frequency drift and not exceed ing  maximum frequency error) - The initial frequency offset when LP-WUR switches on can be set to the [FFS: maximum frequency error or a random value within the maximum frequency error] following the parameters of the oscillator option 1/2/3/4. - When LP-WUR is synced with LP-SS/SSB or MR is used to assist to calibrate LP-WUR to correct the time/frequency error, residual frequency error Fr is assumed at the time when the synchronization/calibration is done.
B556 R2: >0.001 | For both LP-WUR OFF and ON - Time offset cumulated in the off period can be calculated based on the parameter of the oscillator option 1/2 or option 3/4. RTC can be used too.  - Frequency offset calculation can follow the parameter of the oscillator option 1/2 or option 3/4 (cumulating based on the second value in the value pair and not exceed ing  maximum frequency error).  When at the time point after LP-WUR is synced with LP-SS/SSB or if MR can assist to calibrate LP-WUR to correct the frequency error - Frequency offset is the Fr, which is residual frequency error from previous synchronization/calibration
B558: Note1:Any additional LO/FLL/PLL could start running during LP-WUR On duration. The power consumption of any of those LO/FLL/PLL is captured in LP-WUR On power
B559: Note3:The clock error (of both RTC and LO) could be improved to be less than max ppm error of option 1,2,3,4 with clock calibation based on sync signal such as LP-SS or preamble.
B560: 6.3Power consumption model
B561: 6.3.1Power model for Main Radio (MR)
B562: Take the following power model for main radio for evaluation in LP-WUS/WUR SI,
B563: -For IoT and wearable cases, reuse TR38.875 power model as baseline.
B564: -For eMBB and other cases, reuse TR38.840 power model as baseline.
B565: -Introduce 'Ultra-deep sleep' power state for main radio of UEs with LP-WUS receiver 
B566: The following power models are used for 'Ultra-deep sleep' power state for main radio for evaluation.
B567: Table 6.3-1: Power model for Main Radio
B568 R0: Power State | Relative Power (unit) | Ramp-up and down transition energy (Note1): (unit multiplied by ms) | Ramp-up time | Time for sync/re-sync
B568 R1: Ultra-deep sleep | 0.015 | For evaluation, at least for FR1 MR ultra-deep sleep state, (Ramp-up and down transition energy, ramp-up time) is as follows, - Alt 1: (15000, 400ms) as baseline - Alt 2: (40000, 800ms) Company to report which alternative they use for which use cases. | For MR, at least for FR1 evaluation, - Number of SSBs for sync/re-sync for MR is up to 10 - Companies to report timeline and energy consumption
B569: Note1: 
B570: -Ramp-up time may consist of the procedure for main radio hardware tune on e.g., boot, memory load and etc., 
B571: -Time for sync/re-sync consists of the procedure for main radio to re-synchronization with the serving gNB etc.,
B572: -Companies can report the assumption of X in the initial evaluation.
B573: -Ramp up and down energy includes power for ramp-up and ramp-down. Energy consumption for sync/re-sync is separately calculated.
B574: The total time for main radio transition from ultra-deep sleep to active/micro sleep state is the sum of ramp-up time and time for sync/re-sync. 
B575: Note 2:The power state transitions in this table refer to transitions between ultra deep sleep state and active / micro sleep state.

B581: -FFS: whether further categorization/sub-categorization is needed and how.
B582: -FFS: Mapping from values to a LP-WUR architecture or LP-WUR mode of operation

B588: Note3:For LP-WUR 'on' state, more than one value within the above range may be used for evaluation (e.g. for a single LP-WUR architecture)

B590: [For WUR Off value 0.02, oscillator options 1, 2 and RTC can be maintained; ]

B592: 7LP-WUR and LP-WUS design
B593: 7.1LP-WUS receiver architectures
B594: This clause includes the studies for LP-WUR architectures. It does not intend to mandate the implementation of any specific type(s) of LP WUR architecture at the UE. Note this does not prevent RAN4 from defining requirements for LP WUR in the normative phase.
B595: For the study on LP WUR architecture, power consumption relative to the deep sleep state of the MR is provided.
B596: -Deep sleep state of non-RedCap UE should be assumed
B597: For the LP WUR architectures analysis, in addition to LP-WUS detection, consider the following functions when necessary:
B598: -Synchronization signal processing and time/frequency synchronization for LP-WUR
B599: -RRM measurement at least for the serving cell
B600: 7.1.1General description of receiver types
B601: Study at least the following three types of receiver architectures for LP-WUR:
B602: -Architecture with RF envelope detection 
B603: -Heterodyne architecture with IF envelope detection
B604: -Homodyne/zero-IF architecture with baseband envelope detection
B605: -Note: The details of each type of receiver architecture are discussed separately.
B606: -Note: Above receiver architectures are considered suitable for OOK modulation. Some of the architectures can be applicable for other modulations such as FSK.
B607: A)RF envelope detection
B608: The architecture with RF envelope detection is based on at least the following diagram for LP-WUR.
B609: -The RF signal is converted into baseband signal directly via an RF envelope detector.
B610: -There is no Local Oscillator (LO) and no Phase-Locked Loop (PLL).
B611: -1 bit or multi-bit ADC is applied.
B612: -Some component(s), e.g., RF LNA and/or BB AMP, can be optionally applied.
B613: -High-Q matching network and/or RF BPF [and/or BB LPF] can be used to suppress adjacent channel interference or interference from legacy NR signals and/or other LP WUS on adjacent subcarriers.
B614: -FFS the support of band and/or carrier tuning
B617: Figure 7.1.1-1: RF envelope detection based LP-WUR diagram
B618: For the architecture with RF envelope detection,
B619: -It can achieve relatively low power consumption due to the removal of LO/PLL.
B620: -Interference suppression for adjacent channel interference requires very high-Q matching network and/or RF BPF, which is challenging due to the high Q values and may require off-chip components.
B621: -Interference suppression for interference from legacy NR signals and/or other LP WUS on adjacent subcarriers, if performed in RF, requires very high-Q matching network and/or RF BPF, which is challenging due to the high Q values and may require off-chip components.
B622: -The support of multiple bands and/or carriers may require multiple high-Q matching networks and/or RF BPFs or multiple off-chip components.
B623: -RF LNA can be applied to improve sensitivity, with the cost of additional power consumption.
B624: -The noise figure can be relatively high.
B625: B)Heterodyne architecture with IF envelope detection
B626: The heterodyne architecture with IF envelope detection is based on at least the following diagram for LP-WUR.
B627: -The RF signal is down converted into IF signal via an RF mixer with a LO. The IF signal is converted into baseband signal via an IF envelope detection.
B628: -There may be one or multiple IF stages depending on design.
B629: -The choice of the LO is one of the major factors that determine the power consumption.
B630: -Lower power consumption can be achieved by relaxing the accuracy and stability requirements of the LO. However, such increased frequency offset and phase noise should be taken into account in the design and evaluation.
B631: -FLL (frequency locked loop) may replace PLL for non-coherent detection.
B632: -1-bit or multi-bit ADC is applied.
B633: -High-Q matching network and/or RF BPF and/or IF BPF [and/or BB LPF] can be used to suppress adjacent channel interference or interference from legacy NR signals and/or other LP WUS on adjacent subcarriers.
B634: -Some component(s), e.g., RF LNA and/or IF AMP and/or BB AMP, can be optionally applied.
B635: -Image rejection filter or an image rejection mixer is required.
B636: -FFS the support of band and/or carrier tuning
B637: -FFS the choice of IF frequency range
B639: Figure 7.1.1-2: Heterodyne architecture with IF envelope detection based LP-WUR diagram
B640: For heterodyne architecture with IF envelope detection,
B641: -For the support of band and/or carrier tuning, the band and/or carrier tuning can be achieved via tuning the LO frequency.
B642: -The matching network and RF BPF for LP WUR may or may not reuse those of the main radio.
B643: -It is more effective and less complex to use IF BPF instead of high-Q matching network and/or RF BPF to suppress adjacent channel interference or interference from legacy NR signals and/or other LP WUS on adjacent subcarriers.
B644: -Using FLL instead of PLL consumes less power, but it may result in larger frequency error. 
B645: -The IF frequency can be properly selected to avoid LO leakage (DC offset) and flicker (1/f) noise.
B646: -Image rejection can be done via either image rejection filter or image rejection mixer.
B647: -Image rejection filter can be done in either RF or IF, which may require high-Q filter.
B648: -Image rejection mixer requires two-branch (I/Q) mixing with good matching in gain and phase, which consumes additional power.
B649: -RF LNA and/or IF AMP can be applied to improve sensitivity, with the cost of additional power consumption.
B650: C)Homodyne/zero-IF architecture with baseband envelope detection
B651: The homodyne/zero-IF architecture with baseband envelope detection is based on at least the following diagram for LP-WUR.
B652: -The RF signal is directly down converted into baseband signal via an RF mixer with a LO. 
B653: -Baseband envelope detection can be done either in analog domain or in digital domain depending on design, which is not explicitly shown in the diagram.
B654: -The choice of the LO is one of the major factors that determine the power consumption.
B655: -Lower power consumption can be achieved by relaxing the accuracy and stability requirements of the LO. However, such increased frequency offset and phase noise should be taken into account in the design and evaluation.
B656: -FLL (frequency locked loop) may replace PLL for non-coherent detection.
B657: -1-bit or multi-bit ADC is applied.
B658: -High-Q matching network and/or RF BPF and/or BB BPF [and/or BB LPF] can be used to suppress adjacent channel interference or interference from legacy NR signals and/or other LP WUS on adjacent subcarriers.
B659: -No image rejection filter is required.
B660: -Some component(s), e.g., RF LNA and/or BB AMP, can be optionally applied.
B662: Figure 7.1.1-3: Homodyne/zero-IF architecture with baseband envelope detection based LP-WUR diagram
B663: For homodyne/zero-IF architecture with baseband envelope detection,
B664: -For the support of band and/or carrier tuning, the band and/or carrier tuning can be achieved via tuning the LO frequency.
B665: -The matching network and RF BPF for LP WUR may or may not reuse those of the main radio.
B666: -It is more effective and less complex to use BB BPF/LPF instead of high-Q matching network and/or RF BPF to suppress adjacent channel interference or interference from legacy NR signals and/or other LP WUS on adjacent subcarriers.
B667: -Using FLL instead of PLL consumes less power, but it may result in larger frequency error.
B668: -It can suffer from LO leakage (DC offset) and flicker (1/f) noise. The impact may be alleviated by using BB BPF in some cases.
B669: -RF LNA can be applied to improve sensitivity, with the cost of additional power consumption.
B670: -The baseband envelope detection can be done in either analog domain (before ADC) or digital domain (after ADC).
B671: D)FSK receiver
B672: Two examples for FSK receiver architectures are shown below:
B673: -Example 1: parallel OOK receivers and a comparator circuit, e.g.,
B674: -Each path can be implemented using either of [the architecture with RF envelope detection,] heterodyne architecture with IF envelope detection, or homodyne/zero-IF architecture with baseband envelope detection.
B676: Figure 7.1.1-4: FSK receiver with parallel OOK receivers and a comparator circuit based LP-WUR diagram
B677: Study the parallel receiver architectures (as examples that can be captured in the TR) for FSK based on the following diagrams:
B678: -Parallel homodyne architecture 
B680: Figure 7.1.1-5: Parallel homodyne architecture
B681: -The observations made for homodyne/zero-IF architecture with baseband envelope detection in clause 7.1.1-(C) are also applicable here.
B682: -Parallel heterodyne architecture 
B684: Figure 7.1.1-6: Parallel heterodyne architecture
B685: -The observations made for heterodyne architecture with IF envelope detection in clause 7.1.1-(B) are also applicable here.
B686: -Note: Other architectures are not precluded.
B687: -The OOK receiver architectures agreed for study in RAN1#110bis-e are also examples that can be captured in the TR
B688: For FSK receiver based on parallel OOK receivers with heterodyne or zero-IF architecture,
B689: -If no interference between the segments' detectors is allowed, the frequency gap between two adjacent frequency segments should not be smaller than two times the maximum frequency offset, and at least two times of the max frequency offsets within the frequency gap should not be used by other DL signals/channels or other WUS signals.
B690: -If some interference between the segments' detectors is allowed, it can be possible to have frequency gap between two adjacent frequency segments smaller than two times the maximum frequency offset, where the gap is not used by other DL signals/channels or other WUS signals.
B692: -Example 2: using an FM-to-AM detector [or an FM detector]
B693: -Alt 1: Use an analog FM-to-AM detector with a similar architecture as for OOK (e.g. heterodyne or zero-IF architecture), except that the envelope detector is replaced by a FM-to-AM detector.
B694: -Analog FM-to-AM detector can be implemented at least in BB or low-IF.
B696: Figure 7.1.1-7: FSK LP-WUS receiver with FM-AM detector- Alt 1
B697: -Alt 2: Use a FM-to-AM detector [or an FM detector] implemented in digital domain after ADC, with a heterodyne or zero-IF architecture.
B698: -Digital FM-to-AM detector implementation can be considered as part of digital baseband processing.
B699: -Here is an example of using zero-IF architecture:
B701: Figure 7.1.1-8: FSK LP-WUS receiver with FM-AM detector - Alt 2
B702: -The FM-AM detector can be implemented using a frequency discriminator, which converts frequency variations into amplitude changes. It can be implemented in either analog domain (as in Alt 1) or digital domain (as in Alt 2).
B703: -One example, as shown in the figure below, is a conventional quadrature FM discriminator. It multiplies received frequency modulated signal with a phase shifted version, followed by a low pass filter. The amplitude of the output signal is proportional to the frequency of the input signal.
B705: Figure 7.1.1-9: FSK LP-WUS receiver with FM-AM detector by using a frequency discriminator
B706: -Note: Other architectures are not precluded.
B707: Study the receiver architectures (as examples that can be captured in the TR) for FSK with frequency to amplitude conversion based on the following diagrams:
B708: -Homodyne architecture with frequency to amplitude conversion
B709: -I/Q branches are required for frequency to amplitude conversion in digital BB.
B711: Figure 7.1.1-10: Homodyne architecture with frequency to amplitude conversion
B712: -Heterodyne architecture with frequency to amplitude conversion
B714: Figure 7.1.1-11: Heterodyne architecture with frequency to amplitude conversion
B715: -Companies provide the exact type of frequency to amplitude conversion being studied.
B716: -Note: Other architectures are not precluded.
B717: Observation for FSK with frequency to amplitude conversion:
B718: -The FSK architectures with frequency to amplitude conversion is applicable to single-SC FSK, but it may be challenging to make the frequency to amplitude conversion work well with multi-subcarrier FSK.
B719: -Note: single-SC FSK refers to the waveform where each frequency segment has a single subcarrier, and multi-subcarrier FSK refers to the waveform where each frequency segment has multiple subcarriers, as described in the agreements for FSK-1 and FSK-2.
B721: For the FSK architectures with frequency to amplitude conversion, the bandwidth between the frequency segments used for FSK transmissions may not be used for other LP-WUSs or legacy NR transmission in order to allow frequency to amplitude conversion to work properly.
B722: E)OFDMA-based signals/channels detection
B723: For OFDMA-based signals/channels, study the receiver architectures based on the following diagrams:
B724: -I/Q branches are required for digital BB processing.
B725: -Digital BB processing may or may not include FFT (companies to provide details on how).
B726: -For sequence-based OFDM signals/channels, digital BB processing includes sequence correlation in either time domain (without FFT) or frequency domain (after FFT).
B727: -Proponent companies should at least provide details on power consumption reduction compared to the MR regarding the RF and digital BB processing.
B728: -Companies are encouraged to provide the break-down for the components.
B729: -The potential power reduction compared to the main radio may come from e.g.:
B730: -Lower performance LNA/amplifier
B731: -Oscillator/PLL with relaxed performance requirements
B732: -ADC with lower sampling rate and smaller bit-width
B733: -Reduced BB processing complexity compared to the MR
B734: -Companies are encouraged to provide the performance analysis corresponding to the considered power consumption considering the impact of e.g. phase noise, I/Q mismatch.
B735: -Companies to report whether the LP WUR is assumed to share components with MR. In case of component sharing, the potential impact on the MR ultra-deep sleep state should be considered.
B736: -Companies to report the possible number of information bits
B737: -In addition, companies should consider the power consumption in the OFF state and the transition energy.
B739: Figure 7.1.1-12: OFDMA-based signals/channels detection
B740: For the receiver architecture for OFDMA-based signals/channels,
B741: -The receiver architectures for OFDMA-based signals/channels can be used for OOK/ASK and FSK modulated LP-WUS
B742: -For sequence-based OFDM signals/channels, one example diagram with time domain correlator (without FFT) for LP-WUS detection is shown below: 
B744: Figure 7.1.1-13 Example for time domain correlator (without FFT) for LP-WUS detection
B745: F)Additional LP-WUR related designs
B746: For the baseband processing of the LP WUR architectures,
B747: -The baseband processing may use Goertzel filters as an alternative for FFT to compute the signals for one or more tones. Tone energy is computed and a detection algorithm is used to detect the presence of LP-WUS. One example diagram is shown below:
B749: Figure 7.1.1-14: Example for using Goertzel filters as an alternative for FFT to compute the signals for one or more tones
B750: -This can be used with the receiver architecture for OFDMA-based signals/channels for OOK-3.
B751: -This can be used with heterodyne receiver architecture with IF envelope detection or the homodyne receiver architecture with baseband envelope detection for [OOK-1]/FSK-2.
B752: 7.1.1aAnalysis of LP-WUR architectures
B753: For the analysis of different LP-WUR receiver types, at least the following aspects have been considered, and the corresponding power consumption and noise figure have been estimated.
B754: -Assumed modulation/waveform/coding
B755: -Presence of a RF LNA / IF AMP / BB AMP, and the corresponding gain, if any
B756: -Local oscillator
B757: -Type of oscillator and the corresponding frequency accuracy/drifting
B758: -Handling of time/frequency impairments (e.g. synchronization signal processing and time/frequency synchronization for LP-WUR)
B759: -Presence of PLL or FLL
B760: -ADC: sampling rate, bit-width
B761: -Assumed signal bandwidth and guard band, and frequency location within a carrier (including whether it is fixed or can be flexible)
B762: -RF/IF/BB filter characteristics (e.g. type of filter, order, cut-off frequency/frequencies), if any
B763: -Baseband processing (e.g., sequence correlation detection / decoding, other signal processing, if any)
B764: -Assumed frequency band(s) and the support of band and/or carrier tuning
B765: -Duty cycle handling of WUS and other signals (if any)
B766: -Interference rejection capability (including both adjacent-channel interference and interference from adjacent subcarriers occupied by legacy NR signals or other LP WUS)
B767: -Handling of inter-cell interference
B768: -Whether there is any mobility support function, e.g. measurement capability
B769: The results are summarized for each receiver architecture considering different waveform candidates (more details can be found in Annex 7.1.1a and the references are in 7.1.1a.4). The analysis is very high level, and the actual power consumption and noise figure are very implementation specific. Note that the power consumption is provided in relative unit, relative to the deep sleep state of the MR for a non-RedCap UE following the non-RedCap UE power model defined in clause 8.1 of TR 38.840.
B770: For the analysis, 
B771: -FR1 is considered.
B772: -Most sources assumed RF LNA is present for improved sensitivity.
B773: -For LP-WUS bandwidth, most sources consider at least 5MHz. Some sources think the analysis is also applicable to bandwidth up to 20MHz.

B838: 7.1.2RAN4 studies of the receiver
B839: 7.1.2.1General
B840: This Clause captures the study outcome of LP-WUR RF impacts. RAN4 focus on FR1 frequency ranges as first priority, 2.6GHz is selected as an example band for evaluation purpose.
B841: For evaluation purpose, RAN4 defines a new term named as guard RB for LP-WUS, which is Granularity of RB for ACS/ASCS protection. Meanwhile, the traditional guardband for NR channel bandwidth defined in Clause 5.3, TS 38.101-1 is unchanged. The LP-WUS guard RB definition can be described differently for ASCS and ACS as following:
B842: -For ASCS guard RB, the required guard RB(s) should be blanked RB(s), which belong to WUS carrier/BW (i.e., WUS signal plus ASCS guard RB(s))
B843: -For ACS guard RB, the required guard RB(s) are RB(s) between WUS signal edge and nearest edge of guardband of channel bandwidth
B844: -It includes ASCS guard RB(s) (if allocated) and RBs (which could be blanked RBs or RBs used for NR transmission) between WUS carrier/BW edge and nearest edge of guardband of channel bandwidth as defined in spec TS 38.101-1
B845: RAN4 agrees there is no need to restrict symmetric guard RBs for interference rejection of WUS, both ACS and ASCS. 
B846: RAN4 suggests overall bandwidth of the wake-up signal in the NR channel i.e., desired signal along with all the required guard RBs shall fit in the maximum transmission bandwidth configuration as defined in Table 5.3.2-1 of TS 38.101-1.
B847:  
B848: (case 1: LP-WUS within large channel)
B850: (case 2: WUS within narrow channel, WUS bandwidth is comparable with channel bandwidth)
B851: Figure 7.1.2.1-1: Definition of the guardband of NR channel and guard RBs for both LP-WUS ACS and ASCS 
B854: 7.1.2.2ACS evaluation
B855: Adjacent channel selectivity (ACS) is a measure of a receiver's ability to receive an NR signal at its assigned channel frequency in the presence of an adjacent channel signal at a given frequency offset from the centre frequency of the assigned channel. ACS is the ratio of the receive filter attenuation on the assigned channel frequency to the receive filter attenuation on the adjacent channel(s). 
B856: In general, NR ACS requirement is derived based on co-existence study to meet max 5% throughput loss metric. While for LP-WUS, the purpose in the study of ACS is not for justification of co-existence between contained LP-WUS and adjacent NR carrier, but to evaluate the required guard RBs in-between the different signals. Given the WUS waveform would be different from NR signal, the traditional throughput loss metric can not be reused. Therefore, RAN4 agrees to focus on the evaluation of required guard RB rather than ACS requirements with co-existence simulation. 
B857: Framework in RAN4 to evaluate ACS and ASCS vs guard RBs:
B858: -ACS and ASCS guard RB can be evaluated based on the following aspects: 
B859: -Typical filter characteristic, e.g., filter order, pass BW, cut-off frequency 
B860: -Guard RB size 
B861: -RF impairment can also be considered 
B862: -RAN4 adopts two approaches for guard RB evaluation, i.e., filter suppression level analysis and link level simulation
B863: -Averaged power attenuation at ACS or ASCS frequency range 
B864: -FFS whether SINR of the wanted signal at detector input is needed
B865: -FFS whether to use ICS instead of ASCS
B866: -FFS Coexistence-simulation-based framework can also be considered
B867: -FFS on details of coexistence study (if needed) of LP-WUS
B868: -Coverage should be considered
B869: RAN4 Considers a limited set of LP-WUS scenarios for study purpose in RAN4, as shown in Table 7.1.2.2-1.
B870: Table 7.1.2.2-1: Common LP-WUS evaluation parameters for ACS
B871 R0: NR RF channel BW | 20MHz/100MHz   for 5MHz WUS
B871 R1: Guardband of NR channel | Unchanged, defined in Clause 5.3.3 in TS 38.101-1. 810kHz for wanted cell1 20MHz (5MHz WUS at edge), and 510kHz for interference cell2 5MHz.
B871 R2: WUS BW within NR channel | Max 5.04 MHz
B871 R3: WUS RB allocation (Note 1) | 24 RB (15kHz SCS) or 12 RB (30kHz SCS) within 5.04 MHz;

B880: Conclusions for RAN4 ACS guard RB analysis:
B881: Based on the analysis above, RAN4 observed that for 5th order filter, the guard RB number for LP-WUS ACS is in the range of 1RB ~ 6RBs for 30kHz SCS, or 2RBs ~12RBs for 15kHz SCS. For lower power, i.e., lower order filters, the required guard RBs may increase. There is no recommendation on which filter order should be considered to the baseline in SI phase.
B882: -As the analysis from companies did not comprehensively consider all impairments, and individual results are missing the impact of one or many of the following: phase noise, non-linearity, frequency error, implementation margin. For the evaluation, companies have used OOK waveform and FSK waveform, other type of WUS signals are not used. Final conclusion on the number of guard RB is left to WI discussion.

B931: It can be observed that 1 RB of 30kHz SCS with 5th order filtering is sufficient for OOK-1, OOK-2, and OOK-4 LP-WUS.
B933: [7B-4]
B934: The 5MHz interferer is 31.5dB higher than the wanted signal for ACS simulation. Simulations for the waveforms in Figure 7.1.2.2-6 have been performed.

B937: For waveform options of OOK-2, FSK-1 and FSK-2, since segmented RB allocations are considered, several narrower filters would be needed to filter out the interference in contrast to a single filter used for OOK-1 and OOK-4.

B946: For the evaluated waveform options of OOK-1, OOK-2, OOK-4, FSK-1 and FSK-2, guard RB with the size of one 30kHz SCS RB could provide necessary protection of LP-WUS from interference of adjacent NR carrier. For 5th order Butterworth filter assumption, it is proposed to reserve 1 RB for 30kHz SCS or 2RBs for 15kHz SCS for protection of LP-WUS from interference of the adjacent NR carrier.
B948: [7B-5], [7B-6]
B949: Simulations were done to evaluate the impact of adjacent channel and adjacent subcarrier interference. The simulation configuration was created closely match with existing RAN4 minimum requirement for adjacent channel selectivity. Minor adaptations needed to be done as 30 kHz SCS was used. The details of the simulation configuration are provided in Table 7.1.2.2-9.

B953: Simulation results are also optimistic as no frequency error, phase noise or non-linearities were modelled. For filter passband BW 3 options were used. With the narrowest passband BW the filter already cuts into WUS with 3 dB attenuation at WUS edges. Wider bandwidths are used to model practical implementation imperfections as well as possibility of missing or simplified calibration for filter corner frequency.
B954: First an example of ACI impact and GB impact are presented in Figures 7.1.2.2-8 and 7.1.2.2-9. 
B956: Figure 7.1.2.2-8: Impact of ACI, 3rd order filter, 4.32 MHz filter BW, WUS at system BW edge with GB = 0 RB
B959: Figure 7.1.2.2-9: Impact of GB, 3rd order filter, 4.32 MHz filter BW, WUS at system BW edge with GBoff meaning GB = 0RB and with GBon meaning 1 RB GB. ACI is on for both curves.
B960: It can be seen that for the chosen example scenario, adding ACI results in extremely poor BER. With no ACI present, there is clear relationship between SNR and BER. Whether guard band of 1 RB = 360 kHz is present or not does not change the results meaningfully. When looking at the SINR vs. SNR, the explanation is clear: when ACI is present, SINR never increase above -7 dB when SNR increases to 10 dB.
B961: Next results are presented for different filter bandwidths and filter orders when ACI is present in Figures 7.1.2.2-10 and 7.1.2.2-11.
B963: Figure 7.1.2.2-10: Left: BER vs. SNR, 4.32 MHz filter BW, WUS at system BW edge, GB = 1 RB; Right: BER vs. SNR, 5 MHz filter BW, WUS at system BW edge, GB = 1 RB
B966: Figure 7.1.2.2-11: Left: BER vs. SNR, 4.32 MHz filter BW, WUS at system BW edge, GB = 0 RB; Right: BER vs. SNR, 5 MHz filter BW, WUS at system BW edge, GB = 0 RB
B967: It can be seen that with 1 RB GB, BER is starting to be borderline acceptable with higher SNR levels only with 5th order filter for which the -3 dB bandwidth is exactly aligned with WUS BW. This is when no phase noise, non-linearities, frequency error, or practical filter implementation aspects are considered. Also with 15 kHz SCS, the NR channel GB would be narrower and performance with the same 360 kHz GB would be worse. Therefore, we conclude that 360 kHz GB at channel edge does not enable low-power low-cost RF implementation of wake-up receiver.
B968: To estimate the required GB we simulated how the performance changes if 3 additional RBs, i.e. additional 1.08 MHz, are used for guard band at channel edge. These RBs carry NR OFDM signal. Together with the WUS GB of 0 or 1 RB, the total separation from system BW edge is up to 1.44 MHz, and this is on top of the NR channel GB of 810 kHz. Results are shown in Figures 7.1.2.2-12 to 7.1.2.2-13.
B970: Figure 7.1.2.2-12: Left: BER vs. SNR, 4.32 MHz filter BW, WUS 3 RB offset from system BW edge, GB = 0 RB; Middle: BER vs. SNR, 5 MHz filter BW, WUS 3 RB offset from system BW edge, GB = 0 RB; Right: BER vs. SNR, 6 MHz filter BW, WUS 3 RB offset from system BW edge, GB = 0 RB
B972: Figure 7.1.2.2-13: Left: BER vs. SNR, 4.32 MHz filter BW, WUS 3 RB offset from system BW edge, GB = 1 RB; Middle: BER vs. SNR, 5 MHz filter BW, WUS 3 RB offset from system BW edge, GB = 1 RB; Right: BER vs. SNR, 6 MHz filter BW, WUS 3 RB offset from system BW edge, GB = 1 RB
B973: In Figure 7.1.2.2-14 and 7.1.2.2-15, BER vs, SNR results are presented for case where LP-WUS is placed 6 RB offset from system BW edge, and when additional 1 RB GB is applied on top of this.
B975: Figure 7.1.2.2-14: 
B976: Left: BER vs. SNR with ASCI and ACI, 4.32 MHz filter BW, LP-WUS 6 RB offset from system BW edge with no RB GB towards ASCS.
B977: Middle: BER vs. SNR with ASCI and ACI, 5 MHz filter BW, LP-WUS 6 RB offset from system BW edge with no RB GB towards ASCS.
B978: Right: BER vs. SNR with ASCI and ACI, 6 MHz filter BW, LP-WUS 6 RB offset from system BW edge with no RB GB towards ASCS.
B981: Figure 7.1.2.2-15: 
B982: Left: BER vs. SNR with ASCI and ACI, 4.32 MHz filter BW, LP-WUS 6+1 RB offset from system BW edge with no RB GB towards ASCS.
B983: Middle: BER vs. SNR with ASCI and ACI, 5 MHz filter BW, LP-WUS 6+1 RB offset from system BW edge with no RB GB towards ASCS. 
B984: Right: BER vs. SNR with ASCI and ACI, 6 MHz filter BW, LP-WUS 6+1 RB offset from system BW edge with no RB GB towards ASCS. 
B985: The results in above Figures show that performance is still far from ideal with lower order filters even with 6 RB offset. When looking at the data, there are some things to consider: while frequency error, phase noise and non-linearities will make the performance even worse, RAN4 allows a significant relaxation to wanted signal level in ACS test. Therefore, the correct SNR range to be looked at would be rather towards the right side of the plots.
B986: Finally Figure 7.1.2.2-16 shows performance with 3rd filter with different offsets from system BW edge.
B988: Figure 7.1.2.2-16: Performance with 3rd order filter at different RB offsets from system BW edge with 4.32 MHz filter BW
B989: The results in Figures 7.1.2.2-8 to Figure 7.1.2.2-13 show that even with total of 1.44 MHz separation from system BW edge, on top of the 810 kHz NR channel GB the SINR with 3rd order filter will not rise above 0 dB. This is with the filter BW matched to WUS BW, no nonlinearities, no frequency error and no phase noise present. If some practical filter implementation aspects are considered and therefore 5 MHz filter BW is considered, only 5th order filter reaches acceptable performance. With 15 kHz SCS the NR channel GB would be narrower, and performance would be worse.
B990: The results in Figures 7.1.2.2-14 to Figure 7.1.2.2-16 further show that more than 2.16 MHz offset from system BW edge, on top of the NR channel GB, is preferred for improved system performance with lower complexity implementation.
B991: When frequency error is considered in addition, there is additional negative impact to performance, as exemplified in Figure 7.1.2.2-17.
B992:  
B993: Figure 7.1.2.2-17:
B994: Left: SINR vs. SNR with ASCI and ACI, 3rd order filter with 4.32 MHz filter BW, LP-WUS 3 RB offset from system BW edge with 1 RB GB towards ASCS. 
B995: Right: SINR vs. SNR with ASCI and ACI, 3rd order filter with 4.32 MHz filter BW, LP-WUS 6 RB offset from system BW edge with 1 RB GB towards ASCS. 
B996: In Figure 7.1.2.2-17 it can be seen that frequency error has a clear negative impact to SINR, and e.g. in case of 6+1 RB offset from adjacent channel, having frequency error in place can result in need of up to 7 dB higher input SNR to reach the same SINR when compared against case with no frequency error. With 3+1RB offset to band edge in left sub-figure, impact of frequency error makes it very difficult to reach SINR range where successful operation would be possible. It should be noted that while LP-WUS receiver is expected to periodically synchronize itself, there will also be long sleep periods during which frequency can drift. Therefore, considering large frequency offsets is not unreasonable.
B997: If full flexibility for WUS location within NR channel bandwidth is needed, the opportunities for low-power implementation are severely limited for envelope detection based RF implementations. Analog filtering is not sufficient if WUS is placed on the RBs closest to channel edge. Even with 4RB, i.e., 1.44 MHz offset with 30 kHz SCS, performance challenges remain unless complex high order filtering with accurate filter BW is used. For 15 kHz, the performance is expected to be worse due to narrower NR channel GB.
B998: To enable digital filtering, analog-to-digital conversion needs to have sufficient dynamic range to digitize also the interfering signal. Increase in ADC complexity results in higher power consumption.
B999: Analog envelope detection architectures will perform poorly or require a complex implementation if WUS is placed immediately adjacent to channel edge. To improve the likelihood of successful operation and allowing possibilities for low-power implementation, greater than 2.16 MHz offset from outermost RB edge at channel edge is preferred, but exact value needs further study. For architectures using digital detection, placing WUS away from channel edge can enable use of simpler RF HW and power savings.
B1000: In case only adjacent subcarrier interference is considered, situation is greatly improved. Figure 7.1.2.2-18 shows impact to performance with 3rd order filter (4.32 MHz filter BW) with and without 1 RB GB between WUS and other NR subcarriers:
B1002: Figure 7.1.2.2-18:
B1003: Left: BER vs. SNR with ASCI only, 3rd order filter with 4.32 MHz filter BW, LP-WUS 3 RB offset from system BW edge. Comparison between 0 and 1 RB GB.
B1004: Right: BER vs. SNR with ASCI only, 3rd order filter with 4.32 MHz filter BW, LP-WUS 6 RB offset from system BW edge. Comparison between 0 and 1 RB GB.
B1005: Based on the results, guard band does not impact the performance and no guard band is required between WUS and other NR subcarriers.

B1012: For ASCS case, phase noise has little impact on the needed guard RB.  For ACS case, the phase noise may degrade the SNR at least 3 dB for filter order of 8 and 10. 
B1013: The number of guard RB within the WUS carrier/BW for ASCS case does not need to be greater than 1 as illustrated in Figure 7.1.2.2-19, increasing the number of the guard RB within the WUS carrier/BW may have penalty on the SNR in Figure 7.1.2.2-20.
B1014: For ACS case, there is a need to shift the WUS away from the adjacent carrier to get more filter attenuation for the adjacent carrier, the number of the shifted RB depends on the WUR filter order and the number of bits per OFDM symbol. For example, it is preferred placing the WUS signal in the middle of 20MHz channel for OOK-2 for WUR with a filter of order 6. For OOK1 and for WUR with a filter of order 6, the WUS signal should be placed with 6 to 10 RB away from the Adjacent carrier channel edge excluding the NR channel guard band. This is illustrated in Figure 7.1.1.2.2-21.
B1015: The observations from ACS simulation impacts the ACS test case design. The ACS is to test the UE receiver selectivity and the requirement is specified with a special test condition, e.g., ACI level, the UL and DL RB placement. From the simulation results, it can be observed that it is necessary to introduce additional offset from the edge of WUS signal to ACI for certain filter design.  As it is not clear whether to test OOK1, OOK2 or OOK4 and which filter order WUR should be implemented, the detailed design of ACS test case could be discussed in work item phase.  
B1016: Observed from the simulation, there is no need to introduce additional guard RB beyond the WUS BW. For ACS, blanking more RB adjacent to the WUS BW does not help to attenuate the leakage power from ACI, shifting the WUS signal away from the ACI helps more to attenuate ACI because the leakage power from ACI much higher than leakage power from adjacent subcarrier.

B1020: Figure 7.1.2.2-20: BLER for OOK1 with phase noise ON/OFF for ASCS case and Guard RB number =1/2, 1 and 1.5 for filter order 4
B1022: Figure 7.1.2.2-21: BLER performance for BFP order =6 and different # of shift RB with ACI = 33 dB
B1024: 7.1.2.3ASCS evaluation
B1025: To evaluate the in-channel interference, RAN4 has defined a new metric as Adjacent Subcarrier Selectivity (ASCS) for evaluation purpose, which is the ratio of the receive filter attenuation on the assigned channel frequency to the receive filter attenuation on the adjacent sub-carrier(s).  
B1026: The ACSC BWinterference is set as 5MHz, for both ~5MHz and ~1.4MHz LP-WUS cases, as a starting point for ASCS evaluation. The same PSD of LP-WUS and NR signal is assumed within the channel.
B1027: As shown in Figure 7.1.2.3-1, WUS at the center of NR channel would be the worst case of ASCS. For ASCS, the required guard RBs belongs to LP-WUR bandwidth.
B1029: Figure 7.1.2.3-1：LP-WUS at the centre of NR channel, only ASCS needs to be considered. 
B1030: Table 7.1.2.3-1: LP-WUS evaluation parameters for WUS at centre
B1031 R0: NR RF channel BW | 20MHz/100MHz   for 5MHz WUS
B1031 R1: Guardband of NR channel | Unchanged, defined in Clause 5.3.3 in TS 38.101-1
B1031 R2: WUS BW within NR channel | Max  5.04 MHz
B1031 R3: WUS RB allocation (Note 1) | 24 RB  (15kHz SCS)   or 12 RB (30kHz SCS)  in 5.04 MHz ; 
B1031 R4: WUS placement within NR channel | case 1: Center; 
B1031 R5: Guard RB size of LP-WUS | 0 RB, 0.5RB,1RB, 2RB, 3RB at each side Symmetric guard RB 
B1031 R6: A S CS interferer | 5MHz 
B1031 R7: Filter characteristic | 2 nd  to 5 th  order Butterworth lowpass@ baseband bandpass@100MHz bandpass@2 .6 GHz
B1031 R8: Filter passband BW | Same as  WUS bandwidth (number of RBs )

B1033: Conclusion for RAN4 ASCS guard RB analysis:
B1034: Based on the following analysis, RAN4 observed that for 5th order filter, the guard RB number for LP-WUS ASCS is in the range of 0RB ~ 1RBs for 30KHz SCS, or 0RBs ~2RBs for 15KHz SCS. Similar number of guard RBs could be applied also with lower filter orders as performance was observed to remain similar with 3rd order filter.

B1062: Less pass bandwidth will lead to better filter suppression capability, so Table 7.1.2.3-5 with less pass bandwidth will have better performance.
B1064: Link-level simulation analysis: 
B1065: [7B-1]
B1066: The detailed parameters are listed in Table 7.1.2.2-3. The simulation results are in Figure 7.1.2.3-2 with consideration of CFO RF impairment.
B1070: Figure 7.1.2.3-2: ASCS guard RB evaluation with CFO RF impairment
B1072: [7B-4]
B1073: Different from ACS, for evaluation of ASCS we consider the PSD for all RBs including both LP-WUS and NR signal is flat and no power boosting is considered for LP-WUS signal. Figure 7.1.2.3-3 shows the ASCS guard band evaluation for several different waveforms, all the simulations are based on 5th order Butterworth filter.
B1075: a) ASCS guard RB evaluation for OOK-1
B1077: b) ASCS guard RB evaluation for OOK-2
B1079: c) ASCS guard RB evaluation for OOK-4
B1081: d) ASCS guard RB evaluation for FSK-1
B1083: e) ASCS guard RB evaluation for FSK-2
B1084: Figure 7.1.2.3-3: ASCS guard RB evaluation
B1085: Less guard RB is required for ASCS compared to ACS between LP-WUS and NR signals. Considering frequency offset impact, guard RB with the size of one 15kHz SCS RB could provide necessary protection of LP-WUS from interference of adjacent NR sub-carriers.
B1087: [7B-7],[7B-9]
B1088: For ASCS, it can be observed that 1st order filter performs worst while and even with adding 6 guard RBs, the link performance only slightly improves., This means that guard RB does not considerably help if filter order is so low in WUR implementation. For higher than 2nd and 3rd order, the needed Guard RB is not obvious. In simulated link performance vs # RB for 3rd order filter in Figure 7.1.2.3-4, the SNR improvement for 2 RB is 0.2 dB.
B1090: Figure 7.1.2.3-4: Link performance for ASCS selectivity with different filter order
B1091: If the filter order is higher than and equal to 2, smaller guard RB (1 to 2 RB) is sufficient between adjacent subcarrier of eMBB signal and a WUS signal. If the filter order is too low, the guard RB does not help to improve the ASCS selectivity.
B1092: To investigate the ADC impact on the ASCS, the below simulation with different ADC setting is assumed. The following ADC parameters are used: 
B1093: ADC number of bit [2, 4, 6,8] and ADC sampling rate [down sampling factor 4, 8, 16, 32]
B1094: It can be observed that generally there is SNR degradation when sampling rate down-sampling factor is increased from 4 to 32 for the same number of ADC bits and the same BPF order. Such SNR degradation is most prominent for ADC 2 bit case where 1.8 dB SNR degradation is observed in 7.1.2.3-5. Higher ADC bits can reduce the SNR degradation. For example, for ADC bits of 4, 6 and 8, the SNR degradation is around 1 dB. With Higher BPF order (>=6), SNR degradation can be further reduced to around 0.5 dB.
B1106: Figure 7.1.2.3-5: Link performance for ASCS selectivity for filter order 4 and 6 when ADC impairment is enabled.

B1131: Based on the above presented data, following observations can be made: One PRB worth of guard RB with 30 kHz SCS is enough, provided the CFO is under ±100 ppm. However, in case of ±200 ppm of residual frequency error, there is a minor improvement in the required SNR by an additional guard RB. Given the low chip rate, filter order doesn't seem to impact performance, as in, given a modulation scheme, number of guard RBs and residual frequency error, increasing the filter order does not provide significant reduction in the required SNR.
B1132: Therefore, in for 30kHz SCS one PRB worth of guard RB can be used.
B1133: 7.1.2.4Phase noise
B1134: At WUR receiver, reciprocal mixing occurs when a high-power level adjacent interferer signal that is present at antenna mixes with the oscillator noise sideband (phase noise).  The reciprocal mixing generates cochannel interference to the WUR receiver passband as illustrated in Figure 7.1.2.4-1.
B1136: Figure 7.1.2.4-1: Reciprocal mixing for ACI case
B1137: 7.1.2.5gNodeB RF impacts
B1138: The power boosting of 3 dB or 6 dB of a WUS signal relative to the average power of a configured NR carrier can improve the WUS signal coverage. It is beneficial to enable the 3dB or 6 dB power boosting at the existing network. Therefore, RAN4 assumes to reuse the legacy RF specification as a starting point. Some company observed that 3 dB or 6 dB power boosting for a 24 RB WUS signal may not be feasible for some of deployed gNB when average power is kept the same before and after power boosting. It is feasible to configure different PSD carriers in a multiple carrier configuration where WUS signal can be placed in a higher PSD carrier among all configured carriers. The amount of boosting is up to manufacturer declaration following the legacy manufacturer declaration. 
B1139: BS manufacturer could declare if power boosting for WUS signal is supported and the boosting level in the range of [0] to [6] dB is considered. Final power boosting level and the condition of power boosting will be decided based on further analysis in WI phase.
B1140: 7.1.2.6Multi-band capability
B1141: RF envelop detection architecture is more appropriate for single-band operation. IF envelop detection and BB envelop detection are more appropriate for multi-bands operation. Multi-band here still means that only one band at a time is being received. Multi-band capability for other architectures is FFS.
B1142: 7.1.2.7Noise Figure
B1143: RAN4 noticed that the assumed NF in RAN1 simulation may be different with RAN4. RAN4 will derive RF requirement based on NF which is feasible from coverage and implementation perspective. RAN4 will focus on sensitivity evaluation instead of specific NF value in the normative work.
B1144: Generally, noise figure and power consumption are competing specifications, it is very challenging to achieve both low noise figure and low power consumption at the same time, and power consumption typically increases when sensitivity is improved. There is a delicate balance between lower power consumption, e.g., higher noise figure, and system performance.
B1145: 7.1.2.8Band operation for LP-WUS
B1146: RAN4 has discussed the band operation for LP-WUS according to the scenarios listed below with the understanding that the associated RF aspects could be analysed in the work item phase, depending on the scope of the related normative work.
B1147: 1)The band for the low-power radio (LR) and main radio (MR) can be the same, e.g., WUS is located within the NR band used for LP-WUR. In this scenario the WUS and NR DL could be multiplexed according to TDM/FDM modes.
B1148: 2)The band for LR and MR can be different, e.g., WUS is located within an NR band for LP-WUR, and another NR band is used for the MR. 
B1149: Additionally, RAN4 has concluded the following aspects from RF perspective, which are provided for information:
B1150: -WUS located in an SDO band in TS 36.101 is proposed by some companies, and RAN4 recognizes that there exist dependencies on other WGs that have not been considered in this study.
B1151: Note:Some companies have expressed a concern that SDO bands may not be IMT bands.
B1152: 7.2LP-WUS design and L1 procedure
B1153: -When evaluating and/or comparing link performance of MC-ASK, MC-FSK, and CP-OFDMA waveforms of LP-WUS at least
B1154: -raw information bit-size
B1155: -Alt 1:
B1156: -average EPRE within the [time]/frequency resources used for LP-WUS (including any guard bands)
B1157: -time/frequency resources used for LP-WUS (including any guard bands)
B1158: -Alt 2:
B1159: -average EPRE within the [time]/frequency resources used for LP-WUS (including any guard bands)
B1160: -FFS: false alarm probability/rate
B1161: -FFS: misdetection probability/rate
B1162: are kept [comparable or fixed]. 
B1163: -Study at least
B1164: -impact of timing error
B1165: -impact of frequency error
B1166: -impact of phase noise and I/Q imbalance, if applicable
B1167: -impact of ADC resolution and sampling rate
B1168: -impact of interference
B1169: -impact of delay spread
B1170: -impact of doppler spread
B1171: -Companies to report
B1172: -how they modelled SINR
B1173: -SNR is calculated as average EPRE divided by power of noise [and interference].
B1174: -whether and how power pooling across and within MR OFDMA symbols is used.
B1175: -FFS: PAPR applicable to LP-WUS
B1176: -time/frequency resources (including any guard bands) for the scheme
B1177: -false alarm probability/rate and misdetection probability/rate
B1178: -receiver architecture type and its relative power consumption 
B1179: -When comparing waveforms of LP-WUS, consider the impact to gNB for each of the waveform generation schemes. Consider whether there is impact to PAPR and a need for additional hardware for WUS.
B1180: 7.2.1Signal design
B1181: 7.2.1.1Waveform
B1182: -Study generation and link performance of multi-carrier (MC)-ASK (including OOK) waveform
B1183: -study techniques to generate waveform by modulating sub-carriers of CP-OFDM symbol, consider up to M bits transmitted per OFDM symbol, where M is FFS. 
B1184: -Note that above does not preclude DFT-S-OFDMA 
B1185: -Study generation and link performance of multi-carrier (MC)-FSK waveforms
B1186: -study techniques to generate waveform by modulating sub-carriers of CP-OFDM symbol symbol, consider up to M bits transmitted per OFDM symbol, where M is FFS.
B1187: -Study link performance of OFDMA-based signals/channels considering at least the existing signal/channel structure (e.g. CSI-RS, SSS)
B1188: -Other signal/channel structures are not precluded
B1189: For MC-ASK or MC-FSK waveform generation, SCS of a CP-OFDM symbol used for LP-WUS generation can be the same as SCS used for other NR transmissions in CP-OFDM symbol overlapping in time with, study whether SCS can be different, also study
B1190: -FDM/TDM multiplexing with other NR transmissions
B1191: -link performance 
B1192: -impact to legacy UEs
B1193: -impact on gNB 
B1194: For waveform generation the following observations are made
B1195: -Flat spectrum in frequency domain provides robustness against frequency selective fading compared to concentrated energy in frequency domain.
B1196: -for OOK-4, sequence before DFT/LS with variation in phase via such as ZC, M-sequence or QAM sequence can achieve more flattened spectrum.
B1197: -Sequences(s) used in LP-WUS symbol generation with different pulse shape or spectral shape may have different performance. 
B1198: -Knowledge of sequence(s) used in LP-WUS waveform generation may improve performance for at least a receiver with I/Q branches
B1199: Further discuss the following potential observations for waveform generation:
B1200: -When DFT is employed in OOK-4 (M>=2), -1/1 alternation in time or frequency shift in frequency domain may be needed to match CP-OFDM generation.
B1201: -Pre-storing of the generated frequency domain samples at gNB may reduce complexity of waveform generation at gNB with memory requirement depending on number of possible combination. This may be up to gNB implementation.
B1202: -Quantization of generated waveform in frequency domain to existing constellation (e.g. 64QAM) has low impact on performance and reduces complexity. This may be up to gNB implementation.
B1203: -Repetition of a sequence(s) used in LP-WUS generation in frequency can be used to improve diversity for MC-OOK and robustness against frequency offsets for MC-FSK.
B1204: 7.2.1.1.1MC-ASK waveform
B1205: For MC-ASK waveform generation, where K is size of iFFT of CP-OFDMA, N is number of SCs used by LP-WUS including potential guard-bands, study further 
B1206: -Option OOK-1: Single-bit in 1 OFDM symbol, SCs of LP-WUS are 
B1207: -OOK=1 means all SCs are modulated
B1208: -OOK=0 means all SCs are zero power (from base-band point of view)
B1211: -Option OOK-2: Parallel M-bit OOK in frequency domain, 
B1212: -N SCs of LP-WUS is further separated into M segments (M=2 in Figure) possibly with guard-bands in-between and/or around 
B1213: -OOK=1 means all SCs in segment are modulated
B1214: -OOK=0 means all SCs in segment are zero power (from base-band point of view)
B1215: -OOK-2 can be received using the agreed receiver architectures for OOK with parallel envelope detection.
B1218: -Option OOK-3: Multi-tone single-bit OOK
B1219: -N SCs of LP-WUS is separated into L segments (L=2 on Figure) without guard-bands in-between segment, but possibly around
B1220: -OOK=1 means 1 sub-carrier (known by UE) of each segment is modulated, rest of SC is zero power (from base-band point of view)
B1221: -OOK=0 means all SCs in all segments are zero power (from base-band point of view)
B1222: -receiver architecture refers to clause 7.1.1-F
B1225: -Option OOK-4: Transform M-bit OOK in time domain 
B1226: -N SCs of OOK-1 are generated by a transformation (DFT/Least square)
B1227: -N' samples are generated from M-bits 
B1228: -signal modification may or may NOT be used
B1229: -truncation or other additional modification may or may NOT be used, if not used, N is the same as N'
B1230: -N' can be the same as K
B1233: -FFS modulated SCs are e.g. QAM symbols, sequences or other signals 
B1234: -Companies to report their assumptions
B1235: -potential guard-band SCs are zero power (from base-band point of view)
B1236: -optionally, 2 additional segments, one always modulated and one always zero power (from baseband point of view) can be transmitted
B1237: -Other options are not precluded (e.g. OOK-1 with multiple bits in one OFDM symbol)
B1239: Study further methods to modulate input signal of the DFT/Least-Square block for OOK-4, and methods to modulate input signal of N SCs for other MC-ASK/FSK schemes
B1240: -study methods with respect to 
B1241: -improving frequency diversity by flattening the spectrum, frequency repetition and frequency hopping
B1242: -impact to dynamic range of RE power in frequency domain
B1243: -FFS: impact to PAPR of generated time domain modulated MC-ASK/FSK symbol
B1244: -improving robustness to timing error necessary spectrum adjustment for compatibility with CP-OFDM generation
B1245: 7.2.1.1.2FSK waveform
B1246: For M-bit MC-FSK generation study further the following options
B1247: -Option FSK-1: N SCs of LP-WUS are separated to M pairs of segments with potential guard-bands in-between and around. 
B1248: -segment comprises one sub-carrier or multiple contiguous SCs
B1249: -in a pair of segments one segment is modulated, other segment is zero power (from base-band point of view)
B1250: -Option FSK-2: N SCs of LP-WUS are separated to 2^M segments with potential guard-bands in-between and around.
B1251: -segment comprises one sub-carrier or multiple contiguous SCs
B1252: -one segment from 2^M segments is modulated, other segments of SCs are zero power (from baseband point of view)
B1253: -M >0
B1254: -N >1
B1255: -Study how to generate segment in time domain, e.g. OOK-1 or OOK-4 
B1256: -Other options are not precluded.
B1257: In FSK2-envelope-IF waveform generation, the N SCs of LP-WUS can be used to generate 2^M segments at the envelope of the LP-WUR's received signal in baseband where each segment comprises one or more tones.
B1258: 7.2.1.1.3OFDM waveform
B1260: 7.2.1.2Bandwidth and location
B1261: For the purpose of study, the BW of one LP-WUS is not greater than X (FFS X is 5 or 20) MHz for FR1, study further 
B1262: -whether BW of LP-WUS is configurable (implicitly or explicitly)
B1263: -size of guard band [FFS: within or outside of BW X], if any 
B1264: -whether there is different X for Idle, Connected, Inactive modes
B1265: FFS: Whether FR2 is included in the scope of LP-WUS SI
B1266: -From RAN1 perspective, LP-WUS and signals/channels used by MR can be within the same FR1 band.
B1267: -At least LP-WUS and signals/channels by MR can be on the same carrier in the band
B1268: -Study further 
B1269: -Whether LP-WUS and signals/channels used by MR can be different carriers in the band 
B1270: -Details on the LP-WUS location within a carrier
B1271: -Band can be different than band of signals/channels used by MR
B1272: -LP-WUS association with BWP
B1273: -LP-WUS can be configurable within guard-band of a band (like NB-IoT)
B1274: -Study RRC connected mode LP-WUS BW, whether same as IDLE/Inactive mode or different 
B1275: At least for IDLE/Inactive mode, at least one BW-size <=5MHz is recommended to be supported for FR1
B1276: -Other BW sizes are not precluded
B1277: -if additional BW-size(s) are recommended to be supported, BW-size can be up to 20MHz
B1278: -LP-WUS bandwidth size (including guard-bands) is assumed to be an integer number of PRBs
B1279: From RAN1 perspective, for multiplexing with other NR signals and channels, it is beneficial if LP-WUS can be flexibly configured within a carrier.

B1302: 7.2.2L1 procedures
B1303: 7.2.2.1RRM measurements
B1304: Study potential measurement metric used for RRM measurements performed by LP-WUR. 
B1305: -examples of measurement metric are signal quality, signal power, detection rate of LP-WUS/synch signal
B1306: -companies to report assumption of signal used for measurements
B1307: For at least RRM serving cell measurement performed by LP-WUR based on reference signal(s), RAN1 identified at least the following metrics for further study and evaluation (including feasibility, complexity, power consumption, etc)
B1308: -LP-RSSI or Energy detection: linear average of total received power over a RSSI resource. 
B1309: -FFS RSSI resource.
B1310: -LP-RSRP: linear average of received power of resource of reference signal(s) or signal(s) parts. 
B1311: -FFS resource of reference signal(s) or signal(s) parts
B1312: -LP-SINR = LP-RSRP/(power of interference and noise) 
B1313: -FFS how to define "power of interference and noise"
B1314: -LP-RSRQ= [N x] LP-RSRP/LP-RSSI, where N is the factor of resource size difference for evaluation LP-RSRP and LP-RSSI. 
B1315: -Accounting AGC accuracy, ADC of at least 4 bits is required. 
B1316: Note: Reference signal for performing measurements can be e.g. SSB (PSS/SSS/PBCH DMRS), LP-WUS-waveform sequence, LP-SS
B1317: Note: The definition of metrics could be further refined based on future study

LP-SS can be designed to be common among UE groups (cell-specific) and such further reduce system overhead. 
B1349: -For LP-WUR that can receive existing PSS/SSS potentially assisted by PBCH DMRS/TRS for synchronization, existing PSS/SSS potentially assisted by PBCH DMRS/TRS may be used for above functionality. 
B1350: -Periodic LP-SS coverage should be equal to or better than that of LP-WUS.
B1351: -For fine time and frequency synchronization, a signal (e.g. preamble) preceding or part of LP-WUS may be used.
